Gun Shops as Local Institutions: Federal Firearms
page=2 block=41814560 (x=2 y=54) width=319.000000 height=10.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Licensees, Social Disorganization, and
page=2 block=41814560 (x=2 y=73) width=251.000000 height=10.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Neighborhood Violent Crime
page=2 block=41814560 (x=2 y=93) width=183.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Trent Steidley, University of Denver
page=2 block=24645376 (x=2 y=128) width=141.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHP+AdvOT0c7211e1.I wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
David M. Ramey, Pennsylvania State University
page=2 block=24645376 (x=2 y=140) width=188.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHP+AdvOT0c7211e1.I wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Emily A. Shrider, Ohio State University

page=2 block=22479504 (x=1 y=164) width=343.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Recently, scholars have argued that local social and economic institutions, such as
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=176) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
bars and payday lenders, can in?uence neighborhood crime outcomes. Drawing on
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=188) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
routine activities theory and social disorganization theory, such research has pos-
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=200) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ited that such institutions can directly affect crime rates by providing potential targets or
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=212) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
encouraging criminal behavior, and can indirectly affect crime rates by discouraging
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=224) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
social control efforts. We draw on this literature, as well as the literature on reactions to
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=236) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
?rearm prevalence, to examine the effects of gun shops on local crime rates. Using data
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=248) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
for the year 2000 drawn from the National Neighborhood Crime Study (NNCS), the Bureau
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=260) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms, and Explosives’ Federal Firearms Licensees Database
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=272) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
(FFLD), and the National Establishment Time-Series Longitudinal Business Database
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=284) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
(NETS), we use hierarchical linear models to examine how the prevalence of gun shops
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=296) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
at the tract and metro level contribute to tract-level rates of violent crime in eighty-nine
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=308) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
large US cities. Our ?ndings indicate that gun shop prevalence in urban areas is associ-
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=320) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ated with increased rates of homicides and robberies, and that these effects are more
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=332) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
pronounced in neighborhoods characterized by residential instability. We conclude with a
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=344) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
discussion of policy considerations and avenues for future research on local institutions.
page=2 block=44859360 (x=2 y=356) width=335.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
......... . . . . . . . . . ................................................................. . . . . . . . ........................
page=2 block=22480332 (x=1 y=371) width=343.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Recent research on the relationship between local institutions and violent crime
page=2 block=44852364 (x=2 y=390) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
has drawn on both routine activities theory and social disorganization theory to
page=2 block=44852364 (x=2 y=402) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
argue that local institutions likely have both direct and indirect effects on crime
page=2 block=44852364 (x=2 y=414) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
rates (Kubrin et al. 2011; Kubrin and Hipp 2014). Institutions can directly in?u-
page=2 block=44852364 (x=2 y=426) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ence crime by providing opportunities and potential victims for criminal behav-
page=2 block=44852364 (x=2 y=438) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ior (Cohen and Felson 1979; Roncek and Maier 1991; Wilcox and Eck 2011).
page=2 block=44852364 (x=2 y=450) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
......... . . . . . . . . . ................................................................. . . . . . . . ........................

266 Social Forces 96(1)
page=3 block=5046276 (x=1 y=1) width=77.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
They can indirectly affect crime rates by sending signals of social (dis)organiza-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=34) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tion, which can affect social tie formation and social control efforts (Peterson,
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=46) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Krivo, and Harris 2000; Kubrin et al. 2011; Kubrin and Hipp 2014).
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=58) width=293.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Such research has focused primarily on institutions that provide ample suit-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=22 y=70) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
able targets and crime victims, such as bars, pawn shops, and payday lenders
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=82) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
(e.g., Roncek and Maier 1991; Kubrin and Hipp 2014). We explore a different
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=94) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
institution that likely affects crime rates: the gun shop.1 Routine activities theory
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=103) width=342.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
suggests that by being a business with desirable goods, gun shops could directly
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=118) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
affect crime by providing targets and victims. Social disorganization theory sug-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=130) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
gests that gun shops could also have a signi?cant indirect effect on neighbor-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=142) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
hood crime by negatively in?uencing perceptions of the neighborhood, sending
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=154) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
signals of disorganization that discourage residents from engaging in social con-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=166) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
trol efforts. Speci?cally, because gun dealers can be viewed as undesirable busi-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=178) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
nesses that are frequently the target of NIMBY (Not in My Backyard) efforts,
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=190) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
the presence of gun shops could suggest that residents do not care enough about
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=202) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the neighborhood to block the shop’s establishment, or that residents were
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=214) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
unable to do so. This could discourage future social control efforts, as such ef-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=226) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
forts may seem futile or unappreciated. Additionally, gun shops signal that guns
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=238) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
are present in a neighborhood, which could raise fear among those uncomfort-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=250) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
able around ?rearms, thus discouraging them from intervening when disorder
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=262) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
occurs. It could also suggest that the area is dangerous—guns are available
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=274) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
because they are needed for protection—which could further discourage infor-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=286) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
mal social control efforts. Subsequently, we would expect to ?nd higher crime
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=298) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
rates in neighborhoods with gun shops, as the associated fear and negative per-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=310) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ceptions might keep residents from engaging in social control efforts, while the
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=322) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
shops themselves might attract crime by providing suitable targets. It is not that
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=334) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the guns sold generate crime, but that the shops trigger processes that lead to
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=346) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
more crime.
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=358) width=51.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
To test the relationship between gun shops and crime, we employ multilevel
page=3 block=246564962 (x=22 y=370) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Poisson models and three unique datasets—the National Neighborhood Crime
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=382) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Study (NNCS), the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms, and Explosives’
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=394) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Federal Firearms Licensees Database (FFLD), and the National Establishment
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=406) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Time-Series Longitudinal Business Database (NETS)—to examine the effects of
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=418) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
gun shop presence on neighborhood crime rates. The NNCS provides tract-level
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
crime data for eighty-nine US cities in 2000. The FFLD is a complete list of all
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=442) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
federally licensed ?rearms dealers in the United States, while NETS provides
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=454) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
data on business locations. These datasets allow us to test whether gun shops
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
are associated with elevated crime rates at the neighborhood level, while also
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=478) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
accounting for neighborhood and gun shop characteristics.
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=490) width=250.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
This study is a potentially signi?cant extension. Most empirical research on the
page=3 block=246564962 (x=22 y=502) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
relationship between gun shops and violent crime has examined larger geographic
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=514) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
units, such as cities and counties (Wiebe et al. 2009), which are too big to accu-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=526) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
rately measure neighborhood effects, or gun shows (Duggan, Hjalmarsson, and
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=538) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Jacob 2010), which are a short-term phenomenon unlikely to have lasting effects.
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=550) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
To our knowledge, this is the ?rst study to examine this relationship at the neigh-
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=562) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
borhood level. Additionally, this research expands our understanding of the role
page=3 block=246564962 (x=11 y=574) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Page: 4 left:1 upper:1
GunShops and Neighborhood Violent Crime 267
page=4 block=2006384644 (x=195 y=1) width=157.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
of local institutions on crime outcomes, not only by examining a different institu-
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=34) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tion—gun shops—but also by examining the relationship nationwide. Existing
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=46) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
research has focused on institutions in a single city. Finally, given that political dis-
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=58) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cussions around ?rearms have grown increasingly heated, studies exploring poten-
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=70) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
tial associations between ?rearms commerce and crime are useful contributions
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=82) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
for policymakers. This is especially true given the paucity of studies on ?rearms
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=94) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
dealers.
page=4 block=22415930 (x=1 y=106) width=32.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=20
Direct Effects of Local Institutions on Crime
page=4 block=16123053 (x=1 y=133) width=246.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Research has established a connection between local institutions and crime rates.
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=154) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
These studies have primarily focused on institutions associated with increased
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=166) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
crime. Such institutions likely raise crime both directly and indirectly (Kubrin
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=178) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
et al. 2011; Kubrin and Hipp 2014). Studies that focus on the direct effects of
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=190) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
crime often draw on routine activities theory. This theory posits that some insti-
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=202) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tutions can facilitate crime by routinely providing potential targets and by
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=214) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
encouraging behaviors that might lead to crime (Cohen and Felson 1979;
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=226) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
Roncek and Maier 1991; Groff and Lockwood 2014).
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=238) width=230.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
While routine activities theory has been used to explain the association
page=4 block=22467674 (x=12 y=250) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
between numerous institutions and crime, bars probably provide the most illus-
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=262) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
trative example of how an institution can directly in?uence crime rates. First,
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=274) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
bars could lead to increased crime by attracting outsiders to the neighborhood,
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=286) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
which can make local social control more dif?cult. Residents are less likely to
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=298) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
successfully intervene when complete strangers cause disorder than when their
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=310) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
neighbors are disorderly (Jacobs 1961; Roncek 1981; Roncek and Maier 1991).
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=322) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Bars can also attract potential criminals (Frisbie et al. 1977), or cause people to
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=334) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
break norms they normally follow when sober (Felson 1986). Finally, bars pro-
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=346) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
vide an attractive target for crime, as both the customers and the establishment
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=358) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
are likely ?ush with cash (Cohen and Felson 1979; Roncek and Maier 1991),
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=370) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and because the customers are more likely to be impaired and thus less capable
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=382) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of defending themselves (Roncek and Maier 1991).
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=394) width=217.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Of the direct effects of bars on crime, the presence of suitable targets is proba-
page=4 block=22467674 (x=12 y=406) width=331.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
bly the most essential. Researchers repeatedly have found that institutions asso-
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=418) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ciated with increased crime have cash-based transactions, which attract robbers
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
(Wright and Decker 1997; Bernasco and Block 2011; Bernasco, Block, and
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=442) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Ruiter 2013; Kubrin and Hipp 2014). Another key characteristic is the number
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=454) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of suitable targets an institution attracts. Institutions can contribute to crime
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
simply by concentrating larger numbers of potential targets in a small area. This
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=478) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
mechanism has been used to partially explain the crime increases associated
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=490) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
with Wal-Mart stores (Wolfe and Pyrooz 2014).
page=4 block=22467674 (x=1 y=502) width=205.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Indirect Effects of Institutions on Crime
page=4 block=14488217 (x=1 y=529) width=221.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
These characteristics of institutions directly in?uence crime rates, but institutions
page=4 block=22429262 (x=1 y=550) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
have other qualities that could indirectly affect crime rates as well. Research con-
page=4 block=22429262 (x=1 y=562) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
sidering the indirect effects of institutions on crime draws on social
page=4 block=22429262 (x=1 y=574) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=10
Page: 5 left:1 upper:1
268 Social Forces 96(1)
page=5 block=5046276 (x=1 y=1) width=77.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
disorganization theory to argue that institutions can signi?cantly affect crime
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=34) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
rates by shaping perceptions of disorganization in the neighborhood (Peterson,
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=46) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Krivo, and Harris 2000; Kubrin et al. 2011; Kubrin and Hipp 2014). According
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=58) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to this theory, residents are less likely to engage in social control efforts—which
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=70) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
help limit crime in an area—when they believe that their neighborhood is disor-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=82) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ganized. Thus, if an institution sends signals of disorder, it could negatively
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=94) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
impact social control efforts, which could lead to elevated crime rates.
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=106) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
Conversely, if an institution signals organization, it could encourage participa-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=118) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
tion in social control efforts and lead to a reduction in crime. Subsequently, if re-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=130) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
sidents think that an institution brings or signals trouble and disorder, it will be
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=142) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
associated with decreases in social control and increases in crime rates, while in-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=154) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
stitutions that send positive signals about the community will be associated with
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=166) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
reductions in crime.
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=178) width=84.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
This application of social disorganization theory stems from modern interpreta-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=22 y=190) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
tions of Shaw and McKay’s (1942) work on juvenile delinquency. Modern social
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=202) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
disorganization theory argues that crime results when residents lack the capacity
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=214) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to engage in social control, or to address problems and disorder within their
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=226) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
neighborhoods (Kubrin and Weitzer 2003). A neighborhood’s capacity for social
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=238) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
control is shaped by social ties and local organizational presence, which allow re-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=250) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
sidents to develop shared norms and expectations of acceptable behavior. Once
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=262) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
these expectations have been established, residents use their social ties and organi-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=274) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
zations to keep undesirable behavior in check. Their unwillingness to engage in
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=286) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
such social control efforts, however, is shaped by their perceptions of the neigh-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=298) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
borhood and other residents’ willingness to maintain it. If residents believe that
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=310) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
their neighbors care about the neighborhood and will work to maintain shared ex-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=322) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
pectations, they will be more likely to intervene when things are awry. If they
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=334) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
believe that the neighborhood is disorganized—that no one cares and that no one
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=346) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
will try to ?x problems—the residents will be less likely to participate in social
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=358) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
control efforts (Sampson, Raudenbush, and Earls 1997; Sampson 2012). These
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=370) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
perceptions of disorder can be shaped by the physical characteristics of the neigh-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=382) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
borhood; dilapidated housing, for example, often sends the signal that the neigh-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=394) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
borhood is in decline, which can then lead to disinvestment (Massey and Denton
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=406) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
1993). Institutions could send a similar message. If a business is undesirable, it can
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=418) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
signal that the neighborhood is in decline, or that no one cares enough to keep
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
such undesirable businesses out of the neighborhood or at least somewhat in order
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=442) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
(Kubrin et al. 2011; Kubrin and Hipp 2014). This could then discourage residents
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=454) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
from engaging in social control efforts.
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=466) width=161.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
The social and demographic characteristics of a neighborhood can also dis-
page=5 block=246564962 (x=22 y=478) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
courage residents from engaging in social control. Shaw and McKay (1942)
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=490) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
argued that social tie formation is in?uenced by neighborhood disadvantage and
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=502) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
heterogeneity. Poverty limits bonding through its association with high rates of
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=514) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
housing turnover (Bursik 1988). People are more likely to know their neighbors
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=526) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
in stable neighborhoods with low population turnover (Guest et al. 2006).
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=538) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Social ties are weaker in ethnically and racially homogeneous neighborhoods
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=550) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and neighborhoods with large immigrant populations, because communication
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=562) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and bonding are more dif?cult when there are language and cultural differences
page=5 block=246564962 (x=11 y=574) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

GunShops and Neighborhood Violent Crime 269
page=6 block=2006384644 (x=195 y=1) width=157.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
(Shaw and McKay 1942). These factors also in?uence the ef?cacy of local orga-
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=34) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=82) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
poverty, or are racially or ethnically heterogeneous (Triplett, Gainey, and Sun
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=94) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
2003). All of these characteristics can contribute to a neighborhood’s level of
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=106) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
disorganization.
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=118) width=69.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=20
The existing level of (dis)organization in a neighborhood could affect how the
page=6 block=22424090 (x=12 y=130) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
neighborhood responds to undesirable institutions. In a study of alcohol outlets,
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=142) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
like bars and lounges, Pridemore and Grubesic (2012) found that the effect of
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=154) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
alcohol outlet density on assault rates was weaker in areas characterized by
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=166) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
social organization. They argued that organized areas were better able to exer-
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=178) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cise informal social control over both the patrons and management of alcohol
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=190) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
outlets, and that they were better able to marshal government resources to main-
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=202) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
tain order. This suggests that levels of (dis)organization—which can be affected
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=214) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
by the institutions themselves—can also moderate the relationship between
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=226) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
unappealing institutions and crime.
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=238) width=150.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
In sum, institutions can directly contribute to crime by providing suitable tar-
page=6 block=22424090 (x=12 y=250) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
gets and attracting strangers to an area, and can indirectly contribute to crime by
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=262) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
sending signals that an area is disorganized, which can discourage residents from
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=274) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
engaging in social control efforts. Neighborhoods with low levels of disadvantage
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=286) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and turnover may be able to draw on economic resources and existing social ties
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=298) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to limit the direct effects of the institution on crime rates. However, disadvantaged
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=310) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
neighborhoods or those that have high levels of residential instability may fare
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=322) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
poorly when an undesirable institution is present, weakening local social control
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=334) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
efforts.
page=6 block=22424090 (x=1 y=346) width=29.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=20
Firearms Dealers and Crime
page=6 block=10292509 (x=1 y=373) width=157.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Although existing research has focused on institutions like bars and payday len-
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=394) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ders, there are other institutions that could be associated with increased crime.
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=406) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Gun dealers provide an interesting case. As businesses, routine activities theory
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=418) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
would predict that gun shops would be associated with elevated crime rates
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
because they provide suitable targets and desirable goods. This makes them simi-
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=442) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
lar to bars and payday lenders. Gun dealers are also like payday lenders in that
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=454) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
they are often seen as undesirable. Social disorganization theory suggests that
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
undesirable institutions lead to crime by disrupting social control efforts.
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=478) width=308.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Despite these similarities, ?rearms dealers are a unique case. Firearms dealers
page=6 block=22486202 (x=12 y=490) width=331.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
have to locate somewhere, despite often being seen as undesirable and a detri-
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=502) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ment to tourism and other businesses (Lewis 2010). This is due to the fact that
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=514) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
?rearms dealers are fundamental to the constitutional right of ?rearm owner-
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=526) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ship, as the 1968 Gun Control Act mandates that all newly manufactured ?re-
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=538) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
arms be sold from businesses with a Federal Firearms License (FFL) (Cook,
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=550) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Molliconi, and Cole 1995; Braga et al. 2002). Because the Constitution protects
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=562) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
gun ownership, and gun owners are required to purchase new ?rearms from
page=6 block=22486202 (x=1 y=574) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Page: 7 left:1 upper:1
270 Social Forces 96(1)
page=7 block=5046276 (x=1 y=1) width=77.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=HOMJHO+AdvOTb4959fff wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
businesses with FFLs, dealers are a necessity in the marketplace, unlike other
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=34) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
undesirable places (e.g., payday lenders) (Winkler 2013).
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=46) width=241.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
In spite of this, gun shops often face NIMBY efforts and have been the target
page=7 block=246564962 (x=22 y=58) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of local ordinances that limit the number and location of gun dealerships within
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=70) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
a city (Calhoun 2014). This negative reaction is likely tied to people’s response
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=82) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to guns. Although recent national public opinion surveys suggest that a majority
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=94) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of Americans think that owning a ?rearm will protect against criminal victimiza-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=106) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tion (Doherty, Kiley, and Weisel 2014), these ?ndings do not hold in urban
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=118) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
areas. Surveys of urban residents consistently ?nd that urbanites feel that guns
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=130) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
make urban environments less safe, and that urbanites are more likely to support
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=142) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
policies that restrict private access to ?rearms than rural residents (Blocher
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=154) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
2013). Thus, in urban areas (the focus of this paper), gun shops are likely to be
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=166) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
unwelcome.
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=178) width=51.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=20
Regardless of the area, however, feelings toward guns are strongly shaped by
page=7 block=246564962 (x=22 y=190) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
fear and concerns over safety. In some cases, the fear comes from the presence of
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=202) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
guns—studies have demonstrated that increased ?rearm prevalence can reduce
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=214) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
feelings of safety (Hemenway, Solnick, and Azrael 1995; Miller, Azrael, and
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=226) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Hemenway 2000). In other cases, acquiring a gun stems from external fears.
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=238) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Individuals are more likely to carry guns when they feel law enforcement and
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=250) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
the criminal justice system are insuf?cient to protect them from crime
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=262) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
(McDowall and Loftin 1983; Young, McDowall, and Loftin 1987). People are
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=274) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
also more likely to consider purchasing a ?rearm for protection (Kleck et al.
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=286) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
2011) and to actually carry a ?rearm when they fear victimization in their com-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=298) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
munity (Lizotte et al. 2000; Molnar et al. 2004; Watkins, Huebner, and Decker
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=310) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
2008; Felson and Pare 2010). Thus, fear likely plays a signi?cant role in percep-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=322) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tions of guns, either as a direct result of the presence of guns in an area, or
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=334) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
because guns are sought as protection.
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=346) width=163.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Such fears could shape residents’ perceptions when they see gun shops in their
page=7 block=246564962 (x=22 y=358) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
community. Seeing a gun shop in the neighborhood signals that “guns are pres-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=370) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ent here,” which could increase fear among those who feel less safe when guns
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=382) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
are prevalent. The presence of a gun shop could also increase fear among those
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=394) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
who turn to guns for protection, if those individuals see the presence of a gun
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=406) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
shop as a signal that other residents feel the need to carry guns, or that guns are
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=418) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
necessary in the area. These fears could discourage people from engaging in
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
social control efforts. Finally, if many urban residents see gun shops as undesir-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=442) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
able, seeing a gun shop in a neighborhood could send the signal that no one in
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=454) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the neighborhood cared enough to stop (or was incapable of stopping) the gun
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
shop from locating there. This can also depress social control efforts by making
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=478) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
it seem that such efforts will be unsuccessful or unappreciated. All of these fac-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=490) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tors could ultimately lead to higher crime rates. Thus, gun dealers likely lead to
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=502) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
increased crime in an area directly, through mechanisms predicted by routine
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=514) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
activities theory—providing suitable targets—and indirectly, through disrup-
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=526) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
tions to social control efforts, as predicted by social disorganization theory.
page=7 block=246564962 (x=11 y=538) width=319.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Of course, not all gun dealers are the same. Many people purchase guns at

page=8 block=22417562 (x=1 y=154) width=337.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Expectations
page=8 block=4785775 (x=1 y=183) width=73.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=29
Following our argument—that gun shops in urban areas provide suitable targets
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=204) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
for crime and can signal disorder in a way that affects social control efforts—we
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=216) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
have three hypotheses. First, we expect that higher gun shop prevalence will be
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=228) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
associated with higher crime rates in urban neighborhoods. Second, we expect
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=240) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
that higher gun shop prevalence in urban areas will be associated with higher
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=252) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
crime rates regardless of gun shop type. Third, given research that indicates that
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=264) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
neighborhood organization limits the effects of bars on local crime rates
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=276) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
(Pridemore and Grubesic 2012), we expect that this effect will be more pro-
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=288) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
nounced in unstable neighborhoods and neighborhoods characterized by
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=300) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
disadvantage.
page=8 block=22436364 (x=1 y=312) width=58.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=20
Data and Methods
page=8 block=6818472 (x=1 y=341) width=104.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMJHN+AdvOT0a81dd96.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

To examine the association between gun shops and crime, we rely on data from the
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=382) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
National Neighborhood Crime Study (NNCS), the Federal Firearms Licensees
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=394) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Database (FFLD), and the National Establishment Time-Series Longitudinal
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=406) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
Business Database (NETS). The NNCS contains Uniform Crime Report (UCR)
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=418) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
data for violent and property crime at the census-tract level in a representative sam-
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ple of ninety-one cities with a population over 100,000 in 1999 (Peterson and
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=442) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Krivo 2010b). It also includes demographic and socioeconomic data at the tract,
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=454) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
city, and metro level. Although there are 9,563 census tracts in ninety-one cities in
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the full NNCS, we restrict our data to the eighty-nine NNCS cities that provided
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=478) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tract-level homicide data, leaving 8,994 tracts in our analysis. We use tracts as a
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=490) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
proxy for neighborhoods.
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=502) width=105.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
The FFLD is a complete listing of federally licensed ?rearms dealers (FFLs) in
page=8 block=22488566 (x=12 y=514) width=331.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the United States. Maintained by the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms, and
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=526) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Explosives (ATF), the FFLD contains information on all companies or indivi-
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=538) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
duals with licenses to manufacture or sell ?rearms and ammunition in the
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=550) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
United States. The FFLD includes address information, which we geocoded and
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=562) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tracted to get shop locations. The full FFLD contains information on nine classes
page=8 block=22488566 (x=1 y=574) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

272 Social Forces 96(1)

of FFL licenses, which include everything from ?rearms dealers to manufacturers
page=9 block=246550274 (x=11 y=34) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
and importers. We only include data on Type 1 and Type 2 Federal Firearms

Licensees—dealers and pawn shops, respectively—as these are the only catego-

page=9 block=246550274 (x=11 y=70) width=159.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
The NETS Database provides “annual snapshots” of Dun and Bradstreet’s
page=9 block=246550274 (x=22 y=82) width=331.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6

page=9 block=246550274 (x=11 y=94) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
establishments were open for business each year. These establishment-level data

provide information on business name, address, and industry classi?cation

(Walls and Associates 2012). We use this information to identify the number of
page=9 block=246550274 (x=11 y=130) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
retail establishments (grocery stores, pharmacies, gas stations, etc.) in each tract.
page=9 block=246550274 (x=11 y=142) width=340.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Dependent Variables
page=9 block=72811856 (x=11 y=170) width=101.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMLJK+AdvOTa7535478.BI wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
The dependent variables are three-year (1999–2001) average counts of tract-
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=190) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
level homicides and robberies. Criminologists commonly use multiyear counts of
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=202) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
crime to minimize the impact of annual ?uctuations in rare events at small levels
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=214) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of aggregation (Krivo, Peterson, and Kuhl 2009). While the NNCS includes
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=226) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
tract-level counts of property crime, aggravated assaults, and forcible rapes, we
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=238) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
focus on homicides and robberies. This is mostly due to data availability. Some
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=250) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
police departments do not report rape data due to local reporting laws and poli-
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=262) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cies, while others exclude aggravated assault due to data quality concerns
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=274) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
(Peterson and Krivo 2010a, p. 130). As a result, including aggravated assault,
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=286) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
rape, and property crimes would substantially reduce the sample size.
page=9 block=246568092 (x=11 y=298) width=294.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Central Independent Variables
page=9 block=105974320 (x=11 y=326) width=147.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMLJK+AdvOTa7535478.BI wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Following Wiebe et al. (2009), the central tract-level independent variable is a
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=346) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
count of licensed ?rearms dealers per 10,000 people within a given census tract.
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=358) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
We take the natural log of this rate (after adding a small constant of 1.0 to all
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=370) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tracts) to account for both skewness and possible nonlinearity in the relationship
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=382) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
between dealers and violence at the local level.
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=394) width=196.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
To account for the qualitative differences between gun shops, we consider dif-
page=9 block=246606636 (x=22 y=406) width=331.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ferent types of FFL holders, differentiating between dealers, pawn shops, and big
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=418) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
box stores, using a count of each type of dealer per 10,000 residents in each
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
tract. The dealer (FFL Type 1) and pawn shop (FFL Type 2) indicators are avail-
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=442) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
able in the FFLD. To account for sporting goods and department stores, we
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=454) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
identi?ed all of the FFL Type 1 licensees that were licensed to operate more than
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
one place of business, and then identi?ed the FFL licenses issued to corporate
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=478) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
entities rather than an individual person. We identi?ed such businesses as “big
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=490) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
box” stores.2
page=9 block=246606636 (x=11 y=499) width=56.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
In line with previous social disorganization research, we include measures of

vantage variable is an index composed of the average of summed z-scores for six

ment careers (reverse-coded); is jobless and in the working age population

cent of the tract population that is foreign born, arrived in the United States

between 1995 and 2000, and does not speak English (? = .95). To account for

Geoda using a ?rst-order queen criterion.

Metro-level Control Variables
page=10 block=9440368 (x=1 y=398) width=144.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMLJK+AdvOTa7535478.BI wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Although the NNCS is a nationally representative sample of large US cities, our
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=418) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
level-2 independent and control variables are measured at the metropolitan level.
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=430) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
We use metros instead of cities because we need to control for the larger context
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=442) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of gun shop presence, and controlling for the concentration of gun shops at the
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=454) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
metro level provides a more accurate measure of how many gun shops are in the
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=466) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
area. While many large cities have enacted local ordinances limiting the number
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=478) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of licensed gun dealers within city limits, these laws do not extend to the suburbs
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=490) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
or townships in the rest of the metropolitan area. Just accounting for the city,
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=502) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
then, could result in a severe underestimation of gun shop presence. For example,
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=514) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
while the city of Chicago only had three licensed ?rearms dealers in 2000, the
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=526) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Chicago metropolitan area had 854 shops. To appropriately account for the
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=538) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
larger context of gun shops, we include a measure of metro-level FFLs, which is
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=550) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
analogous to the tract-level variable. Results of all models with all level-2 variables
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=562) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
measured at the city level yielded similar ?ndings and are available by request.
page=10 block=22480610 (x=1 y=574) width=324.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4

Analytic Strategy
page=11 block=59837328 (x=11 y=370) width=83.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMLJK+AdvOTa7535478.BI wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
To examine rates of neighborhood violence, this paper employs separate multi-
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=390) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
level models for homicide and robbery using HLM 7.01 with tracts at level 1
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=402) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
and metropolitan areas at level 2. Because homicides and robberies are rare
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=414) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
events within small units, we use nonlinear Poisson models, specifying these
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=426) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
counts with variable exposure by tract population. This strategy is the empirical
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=438) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
equivalent of analyzing rates of neighborhood homicide and robbery (Osgood
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=450) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
2000).3 Additionally, the variances of our dependent variables are considerably
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=459) width=342.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
larger than their means, violating a common assumption of the Poisson model.
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=474) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Controlling for this overdispersion at level 1 in HLM is analogous to a negative
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=486) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
binomial model. All continuous control variables are grand-mean centered in
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=498) width=342.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
the analysis.
page=11 block=246595182 (x=11 y=510) width=52.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Findings

page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=395) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
2014). Additionally, the majority of Americans (56 percent) now think that the
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=407) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
United States would be safer if more people carried concealed weapons
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=419) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
(Newport 2015). Although urban residents remain more skeptical of gun rights
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=431) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
than suburban or rural residents (Blocher 2013), temporal shifts in opinions on
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=443) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
?rearms could change the relationship between gun shops and crime, especially
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=455) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
given the role of perceptions in our theory.
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=467) width=181.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
To address this concern, we have added a more recent time point.
page=27 block=246627282 (x=22 y=479) width=331.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
Unfortunately, there are no recent neighborhood crime datasets as large as the
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=491) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
NNCS. To our knowledge, the most recent large-scale data collection effort is
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=503) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
from a multi-city study on robberies and burglaries for the years 2006–20099
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=513) width=342.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
(Baumer et al. 2014). We replicate our models on a subsample of this more
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=527) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
recent data using tracts from metropolitan areas included in the NNCS, 2005
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=539) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
data from the FFLD, and the 2005–2009 American Community Survey to cap-
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=551) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ture our social disorganization and control variables. We present results of these
page=27 block=246627282 (x=11 y=563) width=342.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=HOMJGN+AdvOTbc475f09 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

models in tables S1 and S2. Notably, the coef?cients for total FFLs and by store

arms in urban areas—the focus of this study—remain negative. Finally, it is pos-

and S2 could be a function of the smaller sample size or characteristics of the
